I don’t know that I’m like… physically restless all the time. But the talking part, yeah, that happens.

She had this whole routine with me at the kitchen table, and I remember hating it because I wanted to go do literally anything else. So yeah, it’s not totally new. It’s just showing up bigger now with work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or have trouble sitting still, or find yourself acting or speaking impulsively without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’m like… physically restless all the time. Like if I make a scheduling mistake, I’ll start explaining what happened, and then I keep explaining it—like way past the point where the other person clearly already understands. I probably made it worse.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from talking too much when nervous, do you often find yourself fidgeting, tapping your hands or feet, or feeling like you need to get up and move around even in situations where you're expected to stay seated, like meetings?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / medical_or_health_services_manager / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Um… I don’t really know. I’m not sure I’ve noticed that as a big thing.

I’m not really sure. I mean, I get worried when I’ve forgotten something or I’m behind, but I don’t know if that’s like… anxiety-anxiety, or just reacting to the mess I made, you know?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I’m not getting fired or anything, and I still show up and do the things I’m supposed to do—mostly. But yes, it has caused problems.

At work, there have been times where a deadline slipped because I didn’t follow up when I said I would, or someone else had to step in and remind a department or make the call I forgot to make. And that’s embarrassing, because in my role you’re supposed to be the person keeping the pieces moving, not the person other people are chasing.

And outside of work too. With community stuff or friends, I’ve had to send those last-minute “I’m so sorry, I completely forgot” messages, which just feels awful. People have been nice about it, mostly, but I can tell sometimes they’re frustrated, or they’ve had to cover for the gap I left. So… yeah, I’m getting by, but it’s not like there are no consequences.

Like if I make a scheduling mistake, I’ll start explaining what happened, and then I keep explaining it—like way past the point where the other person clearly already understands. I can see it on their face sometimes, like, “Okay, got it,” and I’m still going because I’m trying to make sure they know I didn’t mean to mess it up.\n\nAnd then afterward I’m like, why did I keep talking? I probably made it worse.\n\nDoctor: Aside from talking too much when nervous, do you often find yourself fidgeting, tapping your hands or feet, or feeling like you need to get up and move around even in situations where you're expected to stay seated, like meetings?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know. I’m not sure I’ve noticed that as a big thing.\n\nLike, in meetings I might shift around or mess with a pen, but I feel like everybody does that, especially when the meeting is going long or someone’s going through a spreadsheet line by line. We have these meetings where it’s supposed to be 30 minutes and then suddenly it’s an hour, and half the time we’re talking about something that probably could’ve been an email—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t usually feel like I have to get up and move. Not in a strong way. It’s more… I might be uncomfortable or bored, but I can stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you also notice yourself losing track of things, forgetting appointments, or struggling to stay organized in your personal life—like at home or in your daily routines?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just work. It shows up with personal stuff too.\n\nLike, I help out with some community events, and I’ll forget to return calls from volunteers, or there’ll be some small errand I said I’d do—pick something up, follow up with somebody—and then it just… disappears from my head. And then later something reminds me and I get that sinking feeling.\n\nThere have been once or twice where a community meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me, like, “Are you coming?” And then I’m scrambling and apologizing. I hate that, because I don’t want people thinking I don’t care or I’m blowing them off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any persistent feelings of sadness, anxiety, or excessive worry recently that might be affecting your concentration?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I get worried when I’ve forgotten something or I’m behind, but I don’t know if that’s like… anxiety-anxiety, or just reacting to the mess I made, you know?\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around sad all the time. It’s more like I get frustrated with myself. And then I’ll spend too long thinking about why I forgot the thing, or whether someone’s annoyed with me—especially with work stuff, because in healthcare management there are so many moving parts, and if one person doesn’t get called back then suddenly three other people are waiting on it.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of drifting. I guess the short answer is I haven’t noticed some big constant sadness or worry driving the concentration problems. It feels more like the concentration thing comes first, and then I worry afterward.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these forgetfulness and follow-through issues have actually caused real problems—like conflicts, missed opportunities, or people being upset with you—both at work and in your personal life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m not getting fired or anything, and I still show up and do the things I’m supposed to do—mostly. But yes, it has caused problems.\n\nAt work, there have been times where a deadline slipped because I didn’t follow up when I said I would, or someone else had to step in and remind a department or make the call I forgot to make. And that’s embarrassing, because in my role you’re supposed to be the person keeping the pieces moving, not the person other people are chasing.\n\nAnd outside of work too. With community stuff or friends, I’ve had to send those last-minute “I’m so sorry, I completely forgot” messages, which just feels awful. People have been nice about it, mostly, but I can tell sometimes they’re frustrated, or they’ve had to cover for the gap I left. So… yeah, I’m getting by, but it’s not like there are no consequences.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I probably made it worse.\n\nDoctor: Aside from talking too much when nervous, do you often find yourself fidgeting, tapping your hands or feet, or feeling like you need to get up and move around even in situations where you're expected to stay seated, like meetings?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know. I’m not sure I’ve noticed that as a big thing.\n\nLike, in meetings I might shift around or mess with a pen, but I feel like everybody does that, especially when the meeting is going long or someone’s going through a spreadsheet line by line. We have these meetings where it’s supposed to be 30 minutes and then suddenly it’s an hour, and half the time we’re talking about something that probably could’ve been an email—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t usually feel like I have to get up and move. Not in a strong way. It’s more… I might be uncomfortable or bored, but I can stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you also notice yourself losing track of things, forgetting appointments, or struggling to stay organized in your personal life—like at home or in your daily routines?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just work. It shows up with personal stuff too.\n\nLike, I help out with some community events, and I’ll forget to return calls from volunteers, or there’ll be some small errand I said I’d do—pick something up, follow up with somebody—and then it just… disappears from my head. And then later something reminds me and I get that sinking feeling.\n\nThere have been once or twice where a community meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me, like, “Are you coming?” And then I’m scrambling and apologizing. I hate that, because I don’t want people thinking I don’t care or I’m blowing them off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any persistent feelings of sadness, anxiety, or excessive worry recently that might be affecting your concentration?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I get worried when I’ve forgotten something or I’m behind, but I don’t know if that’s like… anxiety-anxiety, or just reacting to the mess I made, you know?\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around sad all the time. It’s more like I get frustrated with myself. And then I’ll spend too long thinking about why I forgot the thing, or whether someone’s annoyed with me—especially with work stuff, because in healthcare management there are so many moving parts, and if one person doesn’t get called back then suddenly three other people are waiting on it.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of drifting. I guess the short answer is I haven’t noticed some big constant sadness or worry driving the concentration problems. It feels more like the concentration thing comes first, and then I worry afterward.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these forgetfulness and follow-through issues have actually caused real problems—like conflicts, missed opportunities, or people being upset with you—both at work and in your personal life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m not getting fired or anything, and I still show up and do the things I’m supposed to do—mostly. But yes, it has caused problems.\n\nAt work, there have been times where a deadline slipped because I didn’t follow up when I said I would, or someone else had to step in and remind a department or make the call I forgot to make. And that’s embarrassing, because in my role you’re supposed to be the person keeping the pieces moving, not the person other people are chasing.\n\nAnd outside of work too. With community stuff or friends, I’ve had to send those last-minute “I’m so sorry, I completely forgot” messages, which just feels awful. People have been nice about it, mostly, but I can tell sometimes they’re frustrated, or they’ve had to cover for the gap I left. So… yeah, I’m getting by, but it’s not like there are no consequences.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting conversations, finishing other people's sentences, or making decisions quickly without really thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure that’s really my thing. I mean, I might jump in sometimes if I think I know where someone’s going, but I don’t think people would describe me as constantly interrupting or making snap decisions.\n\nActually, I tend to go the other way sometimes—like I’ll sit on a decision too long because I’m trying to remember all the pieces, and then… well, now I’m getting off track. But no, the interrupting or blurting stuff hasn’t stood out to me the way the forgetting and follow-through problems have.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yeah, that does happen. It’s kind of embarrassing because I’ll be looking right at the person, like I’m listening, and then my brain just… grabs onto something else.

In meetings, for example, someone will be giving a budget update, and I really do mean to follow it, but then a phone vibrates, or there are people talking out in the hallway, or I suddenly remember something I forgot to do for the league, and I’m gone. Not physically, obviously, but I’ll miss the next few minutes and then come back in not totally sure what they just said.

And then I’m trying to piece it together from context, which sometimes works, but sometimes I have to ask them to repeat it. Or I just nod along for a second, which I know isn’t great.

It’s getting frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this kind of difficulty focusing and putting off tasks going back to when you were a kid or in school, or is this something that's really only become a problem more recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely goes back. I mean, I didn’t think of it that way at the time—I was just a kid—but in elementary school my teachers would tell my parents I was bright, but I was always kind of… off in my own head.\n\nI’d lose worksheets all the time, and homework would just not make it back to school unless my mom basically checked my backpack every night. She had this whole routine with me at the kitchen table, and I remember hating it because I wanted to go do literally anything else. So yeah, it’s not totally new. It’s just showing up bigger now with work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or have trouble sitting still, or find yourself acting or speaking impulsively without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’m like… physically restless all the time. But the talking part, yeah, that happens.\n\nUsually it’s when I’m nervous because I dropped the ball on something. Like if I make a scheduling mistake, I’ll start explaining what happened, and then I keep explaining it—like way past the point where the other person clearly already understands. I can see it on their face sometimes, like, “Okay, got it,” and I’m still going because I’m trying to make sure they know I didn’t mean to mess it up.\n\nAnd then afterward I’m like, why did I keep talking? I probably made it worse.\n\nDoctor: Aside from talking too much when nervous, do you often find yourself fidgeting, tapping your hands or feet, or feeling like you need to get up and move around even in situations where you're expected to stay seated, like meetings?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know. I’m not sure I’ve noticed that as a big thing.\n\nLike, in meetings I might shift around or mess with a pen, but I feel like everybody does that, especially when the meeting is going long or someone’s going through a spreadsheet line by line. We have these meetings where it’s supposed to be 30 minutes and then suddenly it’s an hour, and half the time we’re talking about something that probably could’ve been an email—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t usually feel like I have to get up and move. Not in a strong way. It’s more… I might be uncomfortable or bored, but I can stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you also notice yourself losing track of things, forgetting appointments, or struggling to stay organized in your personal life—like at home or in your daily routines?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not just work. It shows up with personal stuff too.\n\nLike, I help out with some community events, and I’ll forget to return calls from volunteers, or there’ll be some small errand I said I’d do—pick something up, follow up with somebody—and then it just… disappears from my head. And then later something reminds me and I get that sinking feeling.\n\nThere have been once or twice where a community meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me, like, “Are you coming?” And then I’m scrambling and apologizing. I hate that, because I don’t want people thinking I don’t care or I’m blowing them off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any persistent feelings of sadness, anxiety, or excessive worry recently that might be affecting your concentration?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I get worried when I’ve forgotten something or I’m behind, but I don’t know if that’s like… anxiety-anxiety, or just reacting to the mess I made, you know?\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around sad all the time. It’s more like I get frustrated with myself. And then I’ll spend too long thinking about why I forgot the thing, or whether someone’s annoyed with me—especially with work stuff, because in healthcare management there are so many moving parts, and if one person doesn’t get called back then suddenly three other people are waiting on it.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of drifting. I guess the short answer is I haven’t noticed some big constant sadness or worry driving the concentration problems. It feels more like the concentration thing comes first, and then I worry afterward.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these forgetfulness and follow-through issues have actually caused real problems—like conflicts, missed opportunities, or people being upset with you—both at work and in your personal life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m not getting fired or anything, and I still show up and do the things I’m supposed to do—mostly. But yes, it has caused problems.\n\nAt work, there have been times where a deadline slipped because I didn’t follow up when I said I would, or someone else had to step in and remind a department or make the call I forgot to make. And that’s embarrassing, because in my role you’re supposed to be the person keeping the pieces moving, not the person other people are chasing.\n\nAnd outside of work too. With community stuff or friends, I’ve had to send those last-minute “I’m so sorry, I completely forgot” messages, which just feels awful. People have been nice about it, mostly, but I can tell sometimes they’re frustrated, or they’ve had to cover for the gap I left. So… yeah, I’m getting by, but it’s not like there are no consequences.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting conversations, finishing other people's sentences, or making decisions quickly without really thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure that’s really my thing. I mean, I might jump in sometimes if I think I know where someone’s going, but I don’t think people would describe me as constantly interrupting or making snap decisions.\n\nActually, I tend to go the other way sometimes—like I’ll sit on a decision too long because I’m trying to remember all the pieces, and then… well, now I’m getting off track. But no, the interrupting or blurting stuff hasn’t stood out to me the way the forgetting and follow-through problems have.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that even when someone is talking directly to you—like in a one-on-one conversation or meeting—your mind starts to wander and you lose track of what they're saying, or people notice you seem distracted or 'somewhere else'?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that does happen. It’s kind of embarrassing because I’ll be looking right at the person, like I’m listening, and then my brain just… grabs onto something else.\n\nIn meetings, for example, someone will be giving a budget update, and I really do mean to follow it, but then a phone vibrates, or there are people talking out in the hallway, or I suddenly remember something I forgot to do for the league, and I’m gone. Not physically, obviously, but I’ll miss the next few minutes and then come back in not totally sure what they just said.\n\nAnd then I’m trying to piece it together from context, which sometimes works, but sometimes I have to ask them to repeat it. Or I just nod along for a second, which I know isn’t great.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yeah, I think it did cause trouble even then. I remember in elementary school, the way teachers would put it to my parents was always like, “He’s bright, but…” and then the “but” was that I was daydreaming all the time, or I’d lose worksheets, or I wouldn’t turn homework in.

And it wasn’t like I didn’t do any of it. Sometimes it was literally in my backpack, just crumpled up in a folder or stuck between papers, and my mom would have to check my backpack every night to make sure things actually got turned in. Which—funny, I can still picture that backpack, it had this awful zipper that always caught on the fabric—but yeah, the point is, if she wasn’t checking, stuff just didn’t make it from home to the teacher. So yes, it was more than just “that’s how he is.” It caused problems at school.

Or I just nod along for a second, which I know isn’t great.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to childhood specifically before age 12, do you remember these attention and forgetfulness problems actually causing you trouble—like losing things, not finishing schoolwork, or getting in trouble for it—rather than just being a personality trait people noticed?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it did cause trouble even then. I remember in elementary school, the way teachers would put it to my parents was always like, “He’s bright, but…” and then the “but” was that I was daydreaming all the time, or I’d lose worksheets, or I wouldn’t turn homework in.\n\nAnd it wasn’t like I didn’t do any of it. Sometimes it was literally in my backpack, just crumpled up in a folder or stuck between papers, and my mom would have to check my backpack every night to make sure things actually got turned in. Which—funny, I can still picture that backpack, it had this awful zipper that always caught on the fabric—but yeah, the point is, if she wasn’t checking, stuff just didn’t make it from home to the teacher. So yes, it was more than just “that’s how he is.” It caused problems at school.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy. Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry. Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. And that’s embarrassing, because in my role you’re supposed to be the person keeping the pieces moving, not the person other people are chasing.\n\nAnd outside of work too. With community stuff or friends, I’ve had to send those last-minute “I’m so sorry, I completely forgot” messages, which just feels awful. People have been nice about it, mostly, but I can tell sometimes they’re frustrated, or they’ve had to cover for the gap I left. Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank. Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.
